37	\title{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters: A Causal Intervention for Routing Collapse in Time Series Foundation Models}

53	\begin{document}
54	
55	\maketitle
56	
57	\begin{abstract}
58	A common time series foundation model (TSFM) recipe pairs a shared, frozen backbone with a task-specific head. Because downstream series mix heterogeneous regimes that benefit from different aggregation topologies, the natural upgrade is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) head: a router over architecturally distinct experts, one selected per input window. We identify a failure mode of this upgrade that arises with instance normalization. Once any backbone layer is unfrozen, the router converges to a one-hot assignment, routing entropy collapses to $0.000$, and only one expert ever receives a gradient update. We characterize and name this failure mode \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}. The mechanism is direct: instance-normalization layers (e.g., RevIN) strip the per-window mean and variance the router needs. A $720$-run sweep across standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, z-loss, entropy regularization, ReLU routing, expert-choice) converges to the same collapsed state, recovering at most $10.9\%$ MSE. This remains $2.7\times$ worse than our causal intervention, because the failure is in the router's input, not its optimization. We formalize the mechanism via a mutual-information decomposition. The resulting signal-ratio statistic, computable before training, predicts dataset vulnerability to collapse (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$) and correctly flags boundary cases. The diagnosis prescribes a one-line \emph{causal intervention}: \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA sets a new SOTA for adapter-based TSFM transfer under a strictly frozen-backbone protocol: it wins all $54/54$ configurations on the primary backbone (6 datasets $\times$ 3 freeze levels $\times$ 3 seeds, $26$--$79\%$ MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter), strictly beats LoRA, TRACE, AdaMix, and full fine-tuning at $p{<}0.001$, and generalizes across four further backbones and an imputation task. Eight causal controls including a vision-modality replication isolate the cause to instance normalization, and the diagnosis predicts a \emph{Frozen Paradox}: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$ — a two-factor gap dominated by the MoE pool's architectural advantage ($26$--$79\%$ at matched freeze level), with a smaller within-architecture freeze effect ($\leq 13\%$, frozen best on $4/6$) causally attributable to gradient co-adaptation (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen} + RDGF + RevIN-disabled full-FT). Applying the same raw-signal principle at the expert level (Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}) closes the gap on $5/6$ LTSF datasets at $H{=}96$ ($107/123$ cells across six backbones $\times$ four horizons).
59	\end{abstract}
60	
61	\section{Introduction}
62	\label{sec:intro}
63	
64	Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, and Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} now provide powerful pretrained representations for time series, with several recent variants pushing toward billion-parameter scales~\citep{lee2024units, rasul2024lagllama, shi2024timemoe, ekambaram2024ttm, liu2026timers1}. Using one of these backbones on a downstream task still requires attaching a lightweight adapter head that maps hidden states to predictions, and the design of that adapter, more than the backbone choice, determines deployment utility.
65	
66	Standard TSFM adapters~\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm, nie2023patchtst, hu2022lora} pool hidden states and project them to the forecast horizon through the same head for every window (Figure~\ref{fig:problem_overview}a). Real time series mix heterogeneous regimes (quiet baselines, seasonal excursions, abrupt shape changes), and instance normalization handles only the scale-and-offset component of that heterogeneity; residual \emph{shape} heterogeneity (attention patterns, temporal structure) persists in the hidden states and is what motivates a per-window mixture of expert adapters: a pool of topologically distinct heads with a learned router that assigns each window to the most suitable one. Keeping the backbone frozen while training only the adapter is also what makes deployment practical: a shared TSFM in GPU memory can serve many tenants through lightweight per-task adapters hot-swapped from host RAM, an arrangement that per-dataset models like DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} cannot match (Appendices~\ref{app:deployment},~\ref{app:benchmark}).
67	
68	This natural mixture-of-experts approach fails on TSFMs that use instance normalization once any backbone layers are unfrozen (Figure~\ref{fig:problem_overview}b). When the standard MoE recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}, routing on hidden states with load balancing, is applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to zero in most unfrozen configurations (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The router learns to send every window to the same expert, and the remaining experts are never used. The mechanism is what we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the per-window mean and variance that the router needs to distinguish input regimes, leaving it with homogenized hidden states. Even with the backbone frozen, hidden-state routing still underperforms; with the backbone unfrozen, the dominant expert receives larger gradients which reshape the backbone to serve it even more, closing a co-adaptation feedback loop within tens of training steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}). The failure is specific to instance normalization: backbones such as Moirai that use LayerNorm instead of RevIN do not collapse (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}). Standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss, ReLU routing, expert-choice) recover at most $10.9\%$ MSE — still $2.7\times$ worse than RR-MoA (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}) — because the problem lies in the router's \emph{input}, not its optimization.
69	
70	Because the diagnosis pinpoints normalization as the cause, the fix principle is direct. \emph{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters} (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ while keeping the backbone strictly frozen; only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable. The same principle predicts a \emph{Frozen Paradox}: unfreezing triggers co-adaptation collapse (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}), so within a fixed MoE architecture frozen should match or beat unfrozen, which we confirm empirically (frozen best on $4/6$, unfrozen-wins bounded at $\leq 13\%$).
71	
72	\paragraph{Contributions.}
73	\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em, itemsep=0.15em, topsep=0.2em]
74	\item We identify \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: an architectural failure mode of MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs that is upstream of the optimization-induced collapses targeted by Switch, ST-MoE, ReMoE, and Expert-Choice. Eight causal controls (RevIN ablation, BatchNorm/GroupNorm swaps, router-input interpolation, vision cross-modality, and others) converge on the normalizer as cause.
75	\item We formalize the mechanism via a mutual-information decomposition (Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) and derive a tractable signal-ratio predictor $R(\mathcal{D})$ that anticipates which datasets benefit from raw routing at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$), including boundary cases where it correctly does not (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$). The predictor generalizes across three normalization families, five backbones, and a vision modality.
76	\item We propose RR-MoA, the \textbf{minimal causal intervention}: changing only the router's input from normalized hidden states to $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$, with no change to the optimizer, the experts, or the frozen backbone. RR-MoA wins $54/54$ cells against AdaMix (the same architecture with collapsed routing), strictly dominates $11$ optimization-side rescue mechanisms ($720$ runs; best rescue recovers $\leq 10.9\%$, RR-MoA $2.7{\times}$ better), and is not subsumed by naive $(\mu,\sigma)$ re-injection ($+42$--$239\%$ MSE; $11/18$ cells re-collapse). Two architecturally distinct variants confirm the principle generalizes beyond RR-MoA's specific router: \textbf{SR-MoA} (self-routed, no external router; outperforms RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets) and \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (expert-level raw-input dual-stream; matches or beats DLinear on $6/6$ datasets, generalizing to $107/123$ cells across six backbones).
77	\end{itemize}

226	\section{Related Work}
227	
228	\textbf{TSFM Adaptation.} The standard recipe attaches a lightweight module to a frozen TSFM and tunes it on downstream data: LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora}, bottleneck adapters~\citep{houlsby2019adapters}, or a linear probe~\citep{goswami2024moment}. Recent variations explore multi-scale finetuning~\citep{qiao2025msft}, importance-based LoRA selection~\citep{li2025trace}, input-representation adaptation~\citep{benechehab2025adapts}, structured pruning~\citep{zhao2025prune}, in-context tuning~\citep{faw2025icf}, and PEFT alternatives for Chronos~\citep{gupta2024beyondlora}; complementary work computes transferability scores for backbone selection (TEMPLATE,~\citealp{zhang2025template}) or provides a unified evaluation suite for general forecasting models (GIFT-Eval,~\citealp{woo2024gifteval}). These works largely vary the finetuning \textit{strategy} (where to inject parameters, what to prune, what to prompt, which transferability score to use) while leaving the per-input adapter \textit{topology} fixed; AdaPTS~\citep{benechehab2025adapts} proposes several adapter variants but selects one per dataset rather than per input window. We instead ask whether the adapter topology should be chosen per input window via routing over heterogeneous experts; the closest precedent in spirit is heterogeneous mixture-of-adapters in the LLM setting, whereas, to our knowledge, no prior TSFM work uses a per-window mixture over distinct adapter topologies.
229	
230	\textbf{MoE in Foundation Models.} Mixture-of-experts routing~\citep{fedus2022switch} has been used \emph{within} TSFM backbones to scale capacity, with experts placed inside the backbone architecture: Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} routes frequency bands across internal experts, and Time-MoE~\citep{shi2024timemoe} uses sparse activation to reach billions of parameters. RR-MoA applies MoE on an orthogonal axis, at the \emph{adapter level} on top of a frozen backbone. The two axes are complementary rather than competing: stacking RR-MoA on Moirai-MoE further improves accuracy (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}).
```

```
230	\textbf{MoE in Foundation Models.} Mixture-of-experts routing~\citep{fedus2022switch} has been used \emph{within} TSFM backbones to scale capacity, with experts placed inside the backbone architecture: Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} routes frequency bands across internal experts, and Time-MoE~\citep{shi2024timemoe} uses sparse activation to reach billions of parameters. RR-MoA applies MoE on an orthogonal axis, at the \emph{adapter level} on top of a frozen backbone. The two axes are complementary rather than competing: stacking RR-MoA on Moirai-MoE further improves accuracy (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}).
231	
232	\textbf{MoE routing collapse and rescue.} Existing diagnoses of routing collapse cluster, broadly, into three threads. \emph{Optimization-side}: load-balancing losses~\citep{fedus2022switch}, z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, and AdaMix's hidden-state router~\citep{wang2022adamix} target training dynamics. \emph{Representation-geometry}: \citet{chi2022representation} attribute collapse to token clustering around expert centroids that emerges \emph{during} routing learning and propose hyperspherical routing scores. \emph{Architectural / paradigm}: \citet{zhou2022expertchoice} invert the token--expert paradigm via Expert-Choice routing, \citet{wang2025remoe} replace top-$k$ with ReLU gating, and \citet{hua2025inputaware} decouple the router from the task objective so that routing decisions reflect input structure rather than task gradients. Our setting differs from all three: the routing signal is structurally absent from the router's input \emph{by design of an upstream architectural normalizer (RevIN)}, before training dynamics, geometry, or routing-paradigm choices can act. Sweeping five rescue families spanning the optimization and routing-paradigm threads (load balancing, z-loss, Expert-Choice, ReLU gating, and entropy regularization) across twelve configurations on MOMENT$+$RevIN, none recover MSE to within $2.7{\times}$ of RR-MoA (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}). Concurrent work in NLP/vision toward router-free MoEs (AoE~\citep{lv2025aoe}, ERMoE~\citep{cheng2025ermoe}, Routing-Free MoE~\citep{liu2026routingfree}) supports the broader view that the router's input matters: in our setting (\S\ref{sec:main_results}), self-gated experts on raw input match RR-MoA, while the same architecture on hidden states collapses. \citet{wang2026myth} proves MoE specialization reflects hidden-state geometry; we extend this upstream by identifying what shapes that geometry on TSFMs.
233	
234	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Normalization in Time Series.} RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} mitigates distribution shift; DishTS~\citep{fan2023dishts} and Non-stationary Transformers~\citep{liu2022nonstationary} partially recover stripped statistics. Concurrently, \citet{berthelier2026revin} independently show that several RevIN components are redundant or detrimental, and \citet{zou2025ibnorm} argue that variance-centric normalization (BatchNorm, LayerNorm, RMSNorm) is suboptimal for representation learning because two representations with identical mean and variance can encode different amounts of task-relevant information. We identify a specific, mechanistic consequence of this principle that has not been characterized in the MoE literature: when the stripped statistics carry the \emph{routing} signal in a downstream MoE adapter, instance normalization causes the router to collapse to a single expert under unfreezing, a failure mode distinct from the optimization-induced collapse studied in standard MoE rescue literature. Section~\ref{sec:method} formalizes this diagnosis and presents the minimal architectural fix it implies.}
235

365	
366	\subsection{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}
367	\label{sec:rrmoa}
368	
369	An \emph{adapter} $g_\phi: \mathbb{R}^{B \times T \times d} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{B \times h}$ maps a pretrained backbone's hidden states (batch $B$, sequence length $T$, hidden dim $d$) to forecasts of horizon $h$, with $n_g \leq 500{,}000$ trainable parameters. (We use lowercase $h$ for forecast horizon to disambiguate from $\mathbf{H}_\mathrm{FM}$ below, the backbone hidden state.) The goal of per-window routing is to select, for each input window, the subset of expert adapters best suited to that window's characteristics, so that a quiet baseline and a volatile spike are handled by different adapter topologies rather than forced through the same head. As shown in the introduction, this fails when the router reads normalized hidden states, because RevIN removes the per-window statistics that distinguish input regimes. \textbf{RR-MoA} avoids this by splitting the information into two streams: the frozen TSFM produces deep embeddings $\mathbf{H}$ for the experts, while a separate lightweight gate reads the \textit{raw, pre-normalization} input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ to decide which experts to activate:
370	\begin{equation}
371	\begin{aligned}
372	\mathbf{Y} &= \sum_{j=1}^{K} \widetilde{w}_j \cdot \text{Expert}_j(\mathbf{H}_\text{FM}), \qquad
373	\mathcal{T} = \mathrm{TopK}\!\bigl(G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}),\, k\bigr), \\
374	\widetilde{w}_j &= \begin{cases}
375	\dfrac{\exp\!\bigl(G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})_j\bigr)}{\sum_{j'\in\mathcal{T}}\exp\!\bigl(G_\psi(\mathbf{X}_\text{raw})_{j'}\bigr)}, & j\in\mathcal{T},\\[4pt]
376	0, & j\notin\mathcal{T}.
377	\end{cases}
378	\end{aligned}
379	\label{eq:rrmoa}
380	\end{equation}
381	where $G_\psi: \mathbb{R}^{T} \to \mathbb{R}^K$ is a small Conv1d\,+\,pooling\,+\,linear gate ($\sim$1.1K params) operating directly on the raw time series, $\mathcal{T} \subset \{1,\ldots,K\}$ is the set of Top-$k$ expert indices, and each $\mathrm{Expert}_j$ is a canonical adapter head. By construction $\sum_j \widetilde{w}_j = 1$ with support only on $\mathcal{T}$, so only $k$ of $K$ experts contribute per sample (the remaining $K{-}k$ are masked out and do not execute). With $K{=}5$, $k{=}2$ we obtain $40\%$ of the dense ($k{=}K$) expert FLOPs while retaining $63\%$ of the dense MSE improvement over the best fixed adapter (\S\ref{sec:topk}). The raw input preserves the temporal statistics (trend, amplitude, volatility) that RevIN strips away, enabling per-sample routing that reflects the physical characteristics of each time series window.
382	
383	\textbf{Expert pool.} We maintain $K{=}5$ topologically distinct expert adapters (mean-pool, last-token, max-pool, attention-pool, Conv1d-pool), spanning the canonical pooling families used in sequence representation learning~\citep{reimers2019sentencebert}. Architectural diversity is critical: replacing the five distinct heads with five identical experts (same topology, different initialization) degrades MSE by $8$--$15\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}). Pseudocode in Algorithm~\ref{alg:rrmoa} (Appendix~\ref{app:setup}). We now empirically validate that this minimal fix recovers routing diversity, beats the comprehensive baseline suite, and that its predictions hold under eight causal controls.
384	
385	\section{Experiments}
386	
387	\subsection{Setup}
388	
389	\textbf{Datasets and tasks.} Six LTSF benchmarks (ETTh1/h2/m1/m2~\citep{zhou2021informer}, Weather, Electricity~\citep{godahewa2021monash}) with chronological splits and channel-wise normalization~\citep{nie2023patchtst}. We evaluate forecasting ($H{=}96$) and imputation ($20\%$ masked).
390	
391	\textbf{Backbones.} Five backbones spanning two normalization families: instance-normalized MOMENT-small/large~\citep{goswami2024moment} (internal RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin}); LayerNorm-only Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} and Moirai-MoE; and Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos} and Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl} as no-instance-normalization negative controls (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}). MOMENT-small is primary. Primary results use a strictly frozen backbone; freeze-level ablations follow.
392	
393	\textbf{Baselines.} Seven baselines: three fixed adapters, LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} (108-run sweep; Appendix~\ref{app:lora_sweep}), TRACE~\citep{li2025trace}, an independent ensemble of 5 experts, AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} (hidden-state MoE), full fine-tuning (all blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs), and a from-scratch DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} calibration anchor.
394	
395	\textbf{Training and reproducibility.} Adam, MSE loss, 15 epochs; 5 seeds for the core grid, 3 for ablations. Backbone dimensions, full hyperparameters, and per-baseline configurations: Appendix~\ref{app:setup}. All code, raw JSON results, routing-weight logs, and a self-verification script (\texttt{verify.py}) that re-derives every numerical claim from raw data accompany the submission.
396	
397	\subsection{Main Results}
398	\label{sec:main_results}
399	
400	RR-MoA's case rests on three claims: that fixed-topology adapters are suboptimal, that the natural MoE remedy collapses on instance-normalized backbones, and that the collapse cannot be recovered by standard rescue mechanisms. We establish each in turn before comparing RR-MoA against a comprehensive baseline suite.
401	
402	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{RR-MoA vs.\ fixed adapters.} Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa} compares Top-2 RR-MoA against the best fixed adapter, using the \emph{same five expert heads} in all rows. Across the primary 6-dataset LTSF grid $\times$ 3 freeze levels $\times$ 3 seeds $=$ \textbf{54 configurations}, RR-MoA wins all 54 with tight standard deviations.}
403

430	
431	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} Frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on all 6 LTSF datasets (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}). \emph{This headline gap is a two-factor contrast} (frozen+MoE vs.\ unfrozen+single-head) that decomposes into two mechanisms with very different effect sizes:
432	\emph{(M1) MoE architectural advantage (dominant, $26$--$79\%$).} The frozen five-expert mixture with learned per-sample routing provides an architectural advantage that a single unfrozen head cannot match even with full parameter access: per-sample expert selection captures input-regime structure that no single topology in our pool represents on its own (the best-fixed-adapter row of Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa} loses by $26$--$79\%$ \emph{at the same freeze level}, isolating M1 from M2).
433	\emph{(M2) Within-architecture freeze advantage (smaller, $\leq 13\%$, non-unidirectional).} Within RR-MoA itself (architecture held fixed), frozen is best on $4/6$ datasets; light unfreezing wins by up to $13\%$ on the other two. The within-architecture freeze effect is therefore real but bounded and direction-mixed; the headline $12$--$79\%$ number is dominated by M1.
434	\emph{Causal evidence for M2 specifically.} Unfreezing triggers gradient co-adaptation (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}): the dominant expert reshapes the shared backbone, collapsing routing, and freezing prevents this loop and preserves expert diversity. The Router-Detached Gradient Flow ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}) unfreezes the backbone but cuts the routing-weighted gradient feedback, degrading MSE by $+35$--$100\%$ on three datasets — a direct counterfactual on the co-adaptation term. Disabling RevIN inside MOMENT recovers full-FT performance by $54$--$61\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}), pinning the mechanism on instance normalization rather than optimization. SR-MoA (a different router architecture) independently replicates the within-architecture pattern (frozen best on $4/6$; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). Even after $90$-config hyperparameter tuning (learning rates $10^{-5}$ to $10^{-6}$, $100$ epochs, cosine schedule with $10$-epoch warmup, layerwise LR decay, weight decay sweep, gradient accumulation up to effective batch $512$), full fine-tuning still loses by $51$--$71\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}), ruling out optimization confounds.}
435	
436	\begin{proposition}[Gradient Co-Adaptation Drives Entropy Collapse]
437	\label{prop:frozen}
438	Consider a $2$-expert MoE with backbone $\mathbf{h}{=}A\mathbf{x}$, router $p{=}\sigma(\mathbf{w}^\top\mathbf{h})$, loss $\ell{=}p\,\ell_1{+}(1{-}p)\,\ell_2$. Under gradient descent on $(A,\mathbf{w})$: \textup{(i)} the dominant expert's loss decreases faster ($\dot{\ell}_k \propto {-}p_k\|\nabla_\mathbf{h}\ell_k\|^2$), and $\dot{p}$ amplifies whichever expert currently has lower loss, creating self-reinforcing collapse toward $p{\in}\{0,1\}$; \textup{(ii)} freezing $A$ removes the co-adaptation feedback through the backbone (full non-collapse additionally requires $(\ell_1{-}\ell_2)$ to flip sign across input regimes, i.e., expert specialization, which is satisfied by the topologically distinct RR-MoA pool). Proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}.
439	\end{proposition}
440	
441	{\looseness=-1 \textit{Remark.} Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen} covers linear $A\mathbf{x}$; Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}b confirms it in the full 8-block Transformer within ${\sim}50$ steps. Independent ensembles are $37$--$46\%$ worse (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}): learned routing, not mere diversity, drives gains.}
442

Extended grid in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_rrmoa}.}

473	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Why does standard MoE routing fail?} To understand the source of RR-MoA's advantage, we examine AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix}, which uses the standard recipe of routing on hidden states. Table~\ref{tab:adamix} shows that AdaMix collapses to a single expert whenever backbone layers are unfrozen: entropy reaches $0.000\pm0.000$ in 7/12 unfrozen configurations; the remaining 5 stay below $0.55$ (vs.\ max ${\approx}1.61$) with high cross-seed variance ($\sigma$ up to $0.71$), indicating stochastic collapse rather than stable partial routing. The green rows reveal the cause: disabling RevIN inside MOMENT recovers entropy from $0.000$ to $0.66$--$1.32$, proving that normalization is the causal mechanism (BatchNorm1d and GroupNorm produce the same collapse; Appendix~\ref{app:norm_generalization}). The causal contrast is sharpest when comparing backbones directly (Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}): under identical last-4 unfreezing on identical data, MOMENT entropy crashes to $0.000$ in ${\sim}40$ steps while Timer-XL (which lacks RevIN) stays at $1.60$ for all $400$ steps, opposite trajectories from the same code.}

492	In contrast, \textbf{RR-MoA} maintains healthy routing entropy ($1.0$--$1.57$) and wins \textbf{54/54} configurations, with MSE improvements of $26\%$ to $79\%$ over the best fixed adapter. Notably, on Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, where the stripped statistics carry little information), RR-MoA does not improve over fixed adapters, exactly as Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} predicts (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}).
493	
494	\textbf{Rescue-baseline sweep.} To rule out that the collapse is merely under-tuned, we sweep five standard MoE rescue families (Switch load-balance~\citep{fedus2022switch}, ST-MoE z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, entropy regularization, ReMoE ReLU routing~\citep{wang2025remoe}, and expert-choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}) across twelve configurations on 6 datasets $\times$ 2 freeze levels $\times$ 5 seeds $= 720$ runs (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}, Appendix~\ref{app:rescue_details}). The best rescue (z-loss $c_z{=}0.01$) reduces MSE by only $10.9\%$, still $2.7{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA. Excessive balancing ($\alpha{=}10.0$) actively hurts MSE ($+67.7\%$). This confirms Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(ii): when routing information is structurally absent from the hidden states, none of the standard rescue mechanisms we tested can compensate.
495	
496	\textbf{Three architectural instantiations of one principle.} The diagnosis says: restore raw-input access wherever normalization destroyed it. The paper proposes three variants, all confirming the same diagnosis from different angles. \emph{(i)}~\textbf{RR-MoA} (\S\ref{sec:main_results}, Figure~\ref{fig:framework}, headline contribution): external Conv1d gate reads $\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw}$ and selects Top-K experts; experts read hidden states $\mathbf{H}$. \emph{(ii)}~\textbf{SR-MoA} (Self-Routed MoA, Figure~\ref{fig:srmoa_arch}, architecture-agnostic validation): no external router, each expert has its own sigmoid gate on $\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw}$. \emph{(iii)}~\textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}, Figure~\ref{fig:residual_ia_arch}, diagnosis-actionability proof): each expert combines a shared NLinear raw-input branch with a gated backbone residual, which closes the DLinear gap.
497	
498	\textbf{The principle is architecture-agnostic (SR-MoA).} To confirm the bottleneck is in the routing \emph{input} rather than the router \emph{architecture}, SR-MoA~\citep{lv2025aoe,cheng2025ermoe,liu2026routingfree} replaces the external Conv1d router with a per-expert sigmoid gate $\sigma_k(\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw})$, with no external router at all. SR-MoA \emph{outperforms} RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets (ETTh1: $0.464$; ETTm1: $0.388$; Weather: $0.222$; 252 runs total), generalizes across six backbones, and independently replicates the Frozen Paradox (frozen best on 4/6 datasets; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). That a different architecture exceeds RR-MoA while sharing only the raw-input principle confirms it is the \emph{principle}, not the specific router design, that drives the gains. The same SR-MoA architecture gating on hidden states degrades by $+87\%$ to $+768\%$ on \textbf{all 6 datasets} ($p{=}0.016$, one-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank). \textbf{Normalization-specificity:} on Timer-XL (no RevIN), the same raw-vs-hidden comparison yields only $+4$--$9\%$ ($15$--$100{\times}$ smaller than on MOMENT), confirming the degradation is normalization-induced. A third architecture-level control, \textbf{AdaMix-Raw} (Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}), replaces AdaMix's hidden-state router with a raw-input router and recovers entropy to $1.41$--$1.57$ and MSE by $41$--$88\%$ on all 12 cells. All three router instantiations (RR-MoA, SR-MoA, AdaMix-Raw) are consistent with the same diagnosis: the bottleneck is normalization, not router design. Table~\ref{tab:causal_controls} summarizes all eight controls.

524	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Why the diagnosis matters more than the architectural label.} Existing diagnoses of MoE routing collapse split, broadly, between \emph{optimization-side} fixes that target training dynamics (load balancing, z-loss; effective when the router input is informative but under-optimized) and a \emph{representation-geometry} account in which routing learning itself encourages token clustering around expert centroids~\citep{chi2022representation}; architectural alternatives that decouple the router from the task objective~\citep{hua2025inputaware} or invert the token--expert paradigm~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice} sit alongside both. Our setting is upstream of all of these: an \emph{architectural inductive bias} (RevIN) strips the per-window statistics that carry the routing signal \emph{before training starts}. Optimization-side rescue mechanisms cannot recover what is structurally absent (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}), and representation-side reparameterizations presuppose information the input never carried. The mechanism decomposes into two distinct gains: \emph{(Q1)} the best fixed adapter on the same RevIN-normalized hidden states still loses to RR-MoA by $26$--$79\%$ (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}), so residual shape heterogeneity survives normalization and rewards diverse experts; \emph{(Q2)} the signal-ratio $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ measures \emph{dispatch}-signal localization in the stripped statistics, which is why dense routing also benefits from raw access and why Traffic ($R{=}0.14$) correctly does not improve. The Pure Raw-MLP MoE ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:raw_mlp_moe}) makes this concrete: when the router goes to near-uniform on raw input, the architecture behaves as a raw-input ensemble, sufficient on Weather/ETTm2/Electricity (DLinear regime) but insufficient on ETT-temperature data where the TSFM contributes complementary nonlinear structure. The durable contribution is the diagnosis, not the architectural label; the choice between ``MoE with raw router'' and ``ensemble with raw input'' is dataset-conditional, we claim only that wherever instance normalization sits between input and router, the routing signal needs to be re-injected from upstream.}

554	RR-MoA outperforms every baseline ($p{<}0.001$). The independent ensemble (same experts, no router) is $36$--$46\%$ worse, confirming learned routing adds value beyond diversity.
555	
556	\textbf{Closing the DLinear gap.} DLinear (49K params, from scratch) outperforms frozen adapters in absolute MSE (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, grey rows). The gap reflects information destruction by the normalization-encoding pipeline (k-NN and Ridge diagnostics in Appendix~\ref{app:diagnostic}), not adapter capacity. As the diagnosis predicts, restoring raw-signal access \emph{at the expert level} closes this gap: \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (a dual-stream architecture in which a shared NLinear raw-input branch is added to each expert's output; Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}, Figure~\ref{fig:residual_ia_arch}) matches or beats DLinear on \textbf{6/6 datasets} at $H{=}96$ (up to $-7.5\%$), generalizing to $107/123$ cells across six backbones. A self-routed variant SR-RIA\textsuperscript{+} (Appendix~\ref{app:sr_ria}) achieves the same 6/6 match-or-beat with $3$ outright wins; a TSFM-free Pure Raw-MLP MoE control (Appendix~\ref{app:raw_mlp_moe}) localizes the TSFM's contribution and rules out the alternative hypothesis that the raw branch alone is doing all the work.
557	
558	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Rawness vs.\ bypass and dose-response.} Replacing the router's raw input with RevIN-normalized input degrades MSE by $60$--$88\%$ while entropy stays high (Table~\ref{tab:router_input}, Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}), confirming the router relies on the \emph{content} of the stripped statistics, not merely the bypass path. A dose-response interpolation $\mathbf{x}_\alpha = (1{-}\alpha)\,\mathbf{x}_\text{raw} + \alpha\,\text{RevIN}(\mathbf{x}_\text{raw})$ reveals a \emph{phase transition} (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}; 150 runs): MSE degrades by $+5.4\%$ for $\alpha{\leq}0.75$ but jumps to $+283\%$ at $\alpha{=}1.0$, while entropy stays in $[1.43, 1.57]$: normalization renders routing uninformative without reducing diversity. The phase transition threshold is quantitatively predicted at $\alpha^* = R(\mathcal{D})/(R(\mathcal{D})+1) \approx 0.40$ for the median benchmark (Appendix~\ref{app:prop2_depth}). A temporal-shuffle ablation confirms routing depends on $(M,\Sigma)$ statistics, not temporal ordering (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}).}
559	
560	
561	\textbf{Why RevIN destroys routing: formal analysis.} The experiments above converge on a single mechanism: instance normalization strips the per-window statistics that the router uses to tell input regimes apart. To pin this down, we ask what information the router actually has access to before and after normalization. Each input window $\mathbf{x}_i$ decomposes into a mean $M_i = \mu(\mathbf{x}_i)$, a scale $\Sigma_i = \sigma(\mathbf{x}_i)$, and a shape $\mathbf{S}_i = (\mathbf{x}_i - M_i)/\Sigma_i$. RevIN maps $\mathbf{x}_i \mapsto \mathbf{S}_i$ and discards $(M_i, \Sigma_i)$, so the question reduces to how much routing information lives in the discarded part. The mutual-information chain rule gives a clean answer.
562	
563	\begin{observation}[Routing Information Loss Under Instance Normalization]
564	\label{thm:mi_decomp}
565	Let $X$ be a random input window, $E \in \{1,\ldots,K\}$ the expert assignment produced by any deterministic router, and $S = (X - M)/\Sigma$ the RevIN-normalized shape.
566	\begin{enumerate}
567	    \item[\textup{(i)}] \textbf{Exact decomposition.} $I(X; E) - I(S; E) = I(M, \Sigma; E \mid S) \geq 0$.
568	    \item[\textup{(ii)}] \textbf{Quantitative bound.} Without any independence assumption,
569	    \[
570	        I(X; E) - I(S; E) \;\geq\; I(M, \Sigma;\, E) \;-\; I(M, \Sigma;\, S),
571	    \]
572	    where $\varepsilon := I(M, \Sigma;\, S) \geq 0$ captures any residual ``leakage'' of the stripped statistics through the shape. Equality holds iff $(M,\Sigma) \perp\!\!\!\perp S \mid E$.
573	\end{enumerate}
574	\end{observation}
575	
576	In the full-information corner case where the router's signal lives entirely in the stripped statistics ($E$ is a deterministic function of $(M,\Sigma)$ and $(M,\Sigma) \perp\!\!\!\perp S$ unconditionally; see Part~(iii) of the proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}), $I(X; E) - I(S; E) = H(E)$: RevIN destroys the entire routing distribution. We therefore use the \emph{signal ratio} $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M) + \mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)] / \overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ as a tractable monotone proxy for the variance contribution of the stripped statistics. The bound is empirically tight: CCA gives $\varepsilon \in [0.09, 1.26]$ nats, cross-validated within ${\pm}0.26$ nats by KSG~\citep{kraskov2004ksg}; a held-out classifier shows normalization retains only $16$--$41\%$ of routing entropy with bound (ii) gap averaging $0.075$ nats (Appendix~\ref{app:mi_tightness}). Training $\alpha \in [0,1]$ as a learnable parameter, gradient descent converges to $\alpha < 0.5$ on all six datasets ($30/30$ runs; Appendix~\ref{app:learnable_alpha}). $R(\mathcal{D})$ predicts the raw-routing benefit at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}); $H(E \mid S)$ alone does not ($\rho{=}{-}0.12$), since routing \emph{potential} in the stripped statistics matters, not the MI loss itself. Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, $\Delta{=}{+}2.9\%$) is correctly predicted as a boundary case; Solar ($R{=}0.06$) is the one outlier (SSR ablation $+43\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}). Full proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}.

653	\label{fig:frozen_paradox}
654	\label{fig:signal_ratio}
655	\end{figure}
656	
657	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Cross-backbone, task and horizon generalization.}\label{sec:cross_backbone}\label{sec:topk} RR-MoA wins all 21 MOMENT-small/large configurations ($26$--$79\%$; Table~\ref{tab:cross_backbone}), 18/18 Moirai ($3$--$31\%$), and 6/6 Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} datasets ($37$--$90\%$; Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}): adapter-level and backbone-internal routing operate on complementary axes. The negative controls predicted by Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} (Chronos and Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, neither using instance normalization) show no collapse and no RR-MoA gain (4/18 within chance; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_backbone}). Top-2 sparse routing retains most of the dense gain at a fraction of the active-expert FLOPs (Appendix~\ref{app:topk}); imputation ($7/8$; Appendix~\ref{app:imputation}) and horizons $H{\in}\{96{,}192{,}336{,}720\}$ (24/24; Table~\ref{tab:horizon}) confirm robustness. \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}) extends to all 6 datasets across 5 backbones and 4 horizons (Table~\ref{tab:residual_ia_plus_mhcb}).}
658	
659	\vspace{-0.6em}
660	\section{Conclusion and Limitations}
661	\vspace{-0.4em}
662	
663	MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail for structural reasons: the normalization needed for distribution shift also strips the per-window statistics a downstream router needs. Raw pre-normalization routing fixes the failure and motivates Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}; $R(\mathcal{D})$ identifies where it applies.
664	
665	\paragraph{Implication.} Routing collapse on instance-normalized backbones illustrates a broader pattern: when a foundation model's input pipeline destroys signal a downstream router needs, optimization-side rescues (load balancing, z-loss, ReLU routing) cannot recover what is structurally absent: the fix is more readily approached by restoring the missing input than by retuning the optimizer. The same mechanism appears in a vision control (Appendix~\ref{app:vision_moe}); the signal-ratio predictor $R(\mathcal{D})$ predicts which datasets benefit from raw routing (Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$, with a held-out boundary case correctly flagged as low-benefit), and generalization to normalization families we did not test is bounded by the assumptions of Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}.
666	
667	\paragraph{Limitations.} Three boundaries scope our claims. \emph{(i)~Normalization specificity.} Our diagnosis applies to backbones with instance-level normalization (RevIN, BatchNorm, GroupNorm); backbones without it (Chronos, Timer-XL) show no collapse and no RR-MoA gain, as Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} predicts. Extensions to attention-based or learned normalizers are not characterized. \emph{(ii)~Signal-ratio boundary cases.} The signal-ratio predictor $R(\mathcal{D})$ is a single monotone proxy for variance contribution; on Solar ($R{=}0.06$) it correctly flags low benefit but is not sharp. Datasets with strong shape-statistics coupling fall outside the tight regime of the bound. \emph{(iii)~Forecasting and imputation only.} We evaluate forecasting and imputation on LTSF benchmarks. Anomaly detection, classification, and irregularly-sampled tasks are deferred to future work.
668	
```

Why the diagnosis matters more than the architectural label. Existing diagnoses of MoE routing
collapse split, broadly, between optimization-side fixes that target training dynamics (load balancing,
z-loss; effective when the router input is informative but under-optimized) and a representation-
geometry account in which routing learning itself encourages token clustering around expert cen-
troids [55]; architectural alternatives that decouple the router from the task objective [56] or invert
the token–expert paradigm [28] sit alongside both. Our setting is upstream of all of these: an archi-
tectural inductive bias (RevIN) strips the per-window statistics that carry the routing signal before
training starts. Optimization-side rescue mechanisms cannot recover what is structurally absent
(Table G.3), and representation-side reparameterizations presuppose information the input never
carried. The mechanism decomposes into two distinct gains: (Q1) the best fixed adapter on the
same RevIN-normalized hidden states still loses to RR-MoA by 26–79% (Table 1), so residual shape
heterogeneity survives normalization and rewards diverse experts; (Q2) the signal-ratio ρ=−0.88
measures dispatch-signal localization in the stripped statistics, which is why dense routing also
benefits from raw access and why Traffic (R=0.14) correctly does not improve. The Pure Raw-MLP
MoE ablation (Appendix P.3) makes this concrete: when the router goes to near-uniform on raw input,
the architecture behaves as a raw-input ensemble, sufficient on Weather/ETTm2/Electricity (DLin-
ear regime) but insufficient on ETT-temperature data where the TSFM contributes complementary
nonlinear structure. The durable contribution is the diagnosis, not the architectural label; the choice
between “MoE with raw router” and “ensemble with raw input” is dataset-conditional, we claim only
that wherever instance normalization sits between input and router, the routing signal needs to be
re-injected from upstream.

RR-MoA outperforms every baseline (p<0.001). The independent ensemble (same experts, no router)
is 36–46% worse, confirming learned routing adds value beyond diversity.
Closing the DLinear gap. DLinear (49K params, from scratch) outperforms frozen adapters in
absolute MSE (Table 4, grey rows). The gap reflects information destruction by the normalization-
encoding pipeline (k-NN and Ridge diagnostics in Appendix M), not adapter capacity. As the
diagnosis predicts, restoring raw-signal access at the expert level closes this gap: Residual-IA+
(a dual-stream architecture in which a shared NLinear raw-input branch is added to each expert’s
output; Appendix P.1, Figure P.1) matches or beats DLinear on 6/6 datasets at H=96 (up to −7.5%),
generalizing to 107/123 cells across six backbones. A self-routed variant SR-RIA+ (Appendix P.2)
achieves the same 6/6 match-or-beat with 3 outright wins; a TSFM-free Pure Raw-MLP MoE control
(Appendix P.3) localizes the TSFM’s contribution and rules out the alternative hypothesis that the
raw branch alone is doing all the work.
Rawness vs. bypass and dose-response. Replacing the router’s raw input with RevIN-normalized
input degrades MSE by 60–88% while entropy stays high (Table F.1, Appendix F), confirming
the router relies on the content of the stripped statistics, not merely the bypass path. A dose-
response interpolation xα = (1−α) xraw + α RevIN(xraw ) reveals a phase transition (Figure D.1;
150 runs): MSE degrades by +5.4% for α≤0.75 but jumps to +283% at α=1.0, while entropy
stays in [1.43, 1.57]: normalization renders routing uninformative without reducing diversity. The

phase transition threshold is quantitatively predicted at α∗ = R(D)/(R(D) + 1) ≈ 0.40 for the

Why RevIN destroys routing: formal analysis. The experiments above converge on a single
mechanism: instance normalization strips the per-window statistics that the router uses to tell input
regimes apart. To pin this down, we ask what information the router actually has access to before and
after normalization. Each input window xi decomposes into a mean Mi = µ(xi ), a scale Σi = σ(xi ),
and a shape Si = (xi − Mi )/Σi . RevIN maps xi 7→ Si and discards (Mi , Σi ), so the question
reduces to how much routing information lives in the discarded part. The mutual-information chain
rule gives a clean answer.
Observation 1 (Routing Information Loss Under Instance Normalization). Let X be a random
input window, E ∈ {1, . . . , K} the expert assignment produced by any deterministic router, and
S = (X − M )/Σ the RevIN-normalized shape.

                     (i) Exact decomposition. I(X; E) − I(S; E) = I(M, Σ; E | S) ≥ 0.
              (ii) Quantitative bound. Without any independence assumption,
                                              I(X; E) − I(S; E) ≥ I(M, Σ; E) − I(M, Σ; S),
                          where ε := I(M, Σ; S) ≥ 0 captures any residual “leakage” of the stripped statistics
                          through the shape. Equality holds iff (M, Σ) ⊥⊥ S | E.

In the full-information corner case where the router’s signal lives entirely in the stripped statistics (E
is a deterministic function of (M, Σ) and (M, Σ) ⊥⊥ S unconditionally; see Part (iii) of the proof
in Appendix D), I(X; E) − I(S; E) = H(E): RevIN destroys the entire routing distribution. We
therefore use the signal ratio R(D) = [Var(M ) + Var(Σ)]/Var(S) as a tractable monotone proxy
for the variance contribution of the stripped statistics. The bound is empirically tight: CCA gives
ε ∈ [0.09, 1.26] nats, cross-validated within ±0.26 nats by KSG [25]; a held-out classifier shows
normalization retains only 16–41% of routing entropy with bound (ii) gap averaging 0.075 nats
(Appendix L). Training α ∈ [0, 1] as a learnable parameter, gradient descent converges to α < 0.5
on all six datasets (30/30 runs; Appendix K). R(D) predicts the raw-routing benefit at Spearman
ρ=−0.88 (p<0.002, n=9; Figure 5); H(E | S) alone does not (ρ=−0.12), since routing potential
in the stripped statistics matters, not the MI loss itself. Traffic (R=0.14, ∆=+2.9%) is correctly
predicted as a boundary case; Solar (R=0.06) is the one outlier (SSR ablation +43%; Appendix F).

is a deterministic function of (M, Σ) and (M, Σ) ⊥⊥ S unconditionally; see Part (iii) of the proof

for the variance contribution of the stripped statistics. The bound is empirically tight: CCA gives
ε ∈ [0.09, 1.26] nats, cross-validated within ±0.26 nats by KSG [25]; a held-out classifier shows
normalization retains only 16–41% of routing entropy with bound (ii) gap averaging 0.075 nats
(Appendix L). Training α ∈ [0, 1] as a learnable parameter, gradient descent converges to α < 0.5
on all six datasets (30/30 runs; Appendix K). R(D) predicts the raw-routing benefit at Spearman

Full proof in Appendix D.
Cross-backbone, task and horizon generalization. RR-MoA wins all 21 MOMENT-small/large
configurations (26–79%; Table N.1), 18/18 Moirai (3–31%), and 6/6 Moirai-MoE [12] datasets
(37–90%; Table N.2): adapter-level and backbone-internal routing operate on complementary axes.
The negative controls predicted by Observation 1 (Chronos and Timer-XL [10], neither using instance
normalization) show no collapse and no RR-MoA gain (4/18 within chance; Appendix N). Top-2
sparse routing retains most of the dense gain at a fraction of the active-expert FLOPs (Appendix I);
imputation (7/8; Appendix J) and horizons H∈{96,192,336,720} (24/24; Table C.1) confirm ro-
bustness. Residual-IA+ (Appendix P.1) extends to all 6 datasets across 5 backbones and 4 horizons
(Table P.7).

                                                                         10
5   Conclusion and Limitations
MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail for structural reasons: the normalization needed
for distribution shift also strips the per-window statistics a downstream router needs. Raw pre-
normalization routing fixes the failure and motivates Residual-IA+ ; R(D) identifies where it applies.

Implication. Routing collapse on instance-normalized backbones illustrates a broader pattern:
when a foundation model’s input pipeline destroys signal a downstream router needs, optimization-
side rescues (load balancing, z-loss, ReLU routing) cannot recover what is structurally absent: the
fix is more readily approached by restoring the missing input than by retuning the optimizer. The
same mechanism appears in a vision control (Appendix H.1); the signal-ratio predictor R(D) predicts
which datasets benefit from raw routing (Spearman ρ=−0.88, p<0.002, with a held-out boundary
case correctly flagged as low-benefit), and generalization to normalization families we did not test is
bounded by the assumptions of Observation 1.

Limitations. Three boundaries scope our claims. (i) Normalization specificity. Our diagnosis
applies to backbones with instance-level normalization (RevIN, BatchNorm, GroupNorm); backbones
without it (Chronos, Timer-XL) show no collapse and no RR-MoA gain, as Observation 1 predicts.
Extensions to attention-based or learned normalizers are not characterized. (ii) Signal-ratio boundary
cases. The signal-ratio predictor R(D) is a single monotone proxy for variance contribution; on
Solar (R=0.06) it correctly flags low benefit but is not sharp. Datasets with strong shape-statistics
coupling fall outside the tight regime of the bound. (iii) Forecasting and imputation only. We
evaluate forecasting and imputation on LTSF benchmarks. Anomaly detection, classification, and
irregularly-sampled tasks are deferred to future work.

References
 [1] Ashish Vaswani, Noam Shazeer, Niki Parmar, Jakob Uszkoreit, Llion Jones, Aidan N. Gomez,
     Lukasz Kaiser, and Illia Polosukhin. Attention is all you need. In Advances in Neural Informa-
     tion Processing Systems (NeurIPS), volume 30, 2017.
 [2] Rishi Bommasani, Drew A. Hudson, Ehsan Adeli, Russ Altman, Simran Arora, Sydney von Arx,

516	C7 & Vision ViT + InstanceNorm & Collapse only happens in time series, not other modalities & ResNet/ViT MoE on CIFAR-10 collapses identically (App.~\ref{app:vision_moe}) {\small$\Rightarrow$} mechanism is modality-general \\
517	C8 & $(\mu,\sigma)$ re-injection & Just appending stripped stats back fixes it & Re-injected hidden states still $+42$--$239\%$ worse, 11/18 still collapsed (App.~\ref{app:reinjection}) {\small$\Rightarrow$} only \emph{raw} pre-normalization input restores routing \\
518	\midrule
519	\multicolumn{4}{@{}p{0.97\textwidth}@{}}{\textbf{Root cause (surviving hypothesis after C1--C8).} The collapse is not intrinsic to MoE (C1), not architecture-specific (C2, C6, C7), not RevIN-specific (C3), not an optimization problem (C4), not a router-design problem (C5), and not fixable by appending statistics post-hoc (C8). The single mechanism consistent with all 8 controls: \textbf{instance normalization removes the per-window $(\mu,\sigma)$ that carries the routing signal}, so the fix must restore it from \emph{raw pre-normalization} input.} \\
520	\bottomrule
521	\end{tabular}
522	\end{table}
523	
524	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Why the diagnosis matters more than the architectural label.} Existing diagnoses of MoE routing collapse split, broadly, between \emph{optimization-side} fixes that target training dynamics (load balancing, z-loss; effective when the router input is informative but under-optimized) and a \emph{representation-geometry} account in which routing learning itself encourages token clustering around expert centroids~\citep{chi2022representation}; architectural alternatives that decouple the router from the task objective~\citep{hua2025inputaware} or invert the token--expert paradigm~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice} sit alongside both. Our setting is upstream of all of these: an \emph{architectural inductive bias} (RevIN) strips the per-window statistics that carry the routing signal \emph{before training starts}. Optimization-side rescue mechanisms cannot recover what is structurally absent (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}), and representation-side reparameterizations presuppose information the input never carried. The mechanism decomposes into two distinct gains: \emph{(Q1)} the best fixed adapter on the same RevIN-normalized hidden states still loses to RR-MoA by $26$--$79\%$ (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}), so residual shape heterogeneity survives normalization and rewards diverse experts; \emph{(Q2)} the signal-ratio $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ measures \emph{dispatch}-signal localization in the stripped statistics, which is why dense routing also benefits from raw access and why Traffic ($R{=}0.14$) correctly does not improve. The Pure Raw-MLP MoE ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:raw_mlp_moe}) makes this concrete: when the router goes to near-uniform on raw input, the architecture behaves as a raw-input ensemble, sufficient on Weather/ETTm2/Electricity (DLinear regime) but insufficient on ETT-temperature data where the TSFM contributes complementary nonlinear structure. The durable contribution is the diagnosis, not the architectural label; the choice between ``MoE with raw router'' and ``ensemble with raw input'' is dataset-conditional, we claim only that wherever instance normalization sits between input and router, the routing signal needs to be re-injected from upstream.}
525	\vspace{-0.3em}

  "old_string": "{\\looseness=-1 \\textbf{Why the diagnosis matters more than the architectural label.} Existing diagnoses of MoE routing collapse split, broadly, between \\emph{optimization-side} fixes that target training dynamics (load balancing, z-loss; effective when the router input is informative but under-optimized) and a \\emph{representation-geometry} account in which routing learning itself encourages token clustering around expert centroids~\\citep{chi2022representation}; architectural alternatives that decouple the router from the task objective~\\citep{hua2025inputaware} or invert the token--expert paradigm~\\citep{zhou2022expertchoice} sit alongside both. Our setting is upstream of all of these: an \\emph{architectural inductive bias} (RevIN) strips the per-window statistics that carry the routing signal \\emph{before training starts}. Optimization-side rescue mechanisms cannot recover what is structurally absent (Table~\\ref{tab:rescue}), and representation-side reparameterizations presuppose information the input never carried. The mechanism decomposes into two distinct gains: \\emph{(Q1)} the best fixed adapter on the same RevIN-normalized hidden states still loses to RR-MoA by $26$--$79\\%$ (Table~\\ref{tab:rrmoa}), so residual shape heterogeneity survives normalization and rewards diverse experts; \\emph{(Q2)} the signal-ratio $\\rho{=}{-}0.88$ measures \\emph{dispatch}-signal localization in the stripped statistics, which is why dense routing also benefits from raw access and why Traffic ($R{=}0.14$) correctly does not improve. The Pure Raw-MLP MoE ablation (Appendix~\\ref{app:raw_mlp_moe}) makes this concrete: when the router goes to near-uniform on raw input, the architecture behaves as a raw-input ensemble, sufficient on Weather/ETTm2/Electricity (DLinear regime) but insufficient on ETT-temperature data where the TSFM contributes complementary nonlinear structure. The durable contribution is the diagnosis, not the architectural label; the choice between ``MoE with raw router'' and ``ensemble with raw input'' is dataset-conditional, we claim only that wherever instance normalization sits between input and router, the routing signal needs to be re-injected from upstream.}\n\\vspace{-0.3em}",
  "new_string": "{\\looseness=-1 \\textbf{Two distinct gains, one diagnosis.} The mechanism decomposes into: \\emph{(Q1)} the best fixed adapter on the same RevIN-normalized hidden states still loses to RR-MoA by $26$--$79\\%$ (Table~\\ref{tab:rrmoa}), so residual shape heterogeneity survives normalization and rewards diverse experts; \\emph{(Q2)} the signal-ratio $\\rho{=}{-}0.88$ measures \\emph{dispatch}-signal localization in the stripped statistics, which is why dense routing also benefits from raw access and why Traffic ($R{=}0.14$) correctly does not improve. The Pure Raw-MLP MoE ablation (Appendix~\\ref{app:raw_mlp_moe}) shows the dataset-conditional choice between ``MoE with raw router'' and ``ensemble with raw input'': wherever instance normalization sits between input and router, the routing signal must be re-injected from upstream.}\n\\vspace{-0.3em}"

full FT unfreezes all 8 blocks. Wilcoxon, Bonferroni-corrected. Grey rows: from-scratch calibration
anchors.

   Best LoRA (108-run sweep)                 1.154 ± 0.000            0.956 ± 0.072          0.600 ± 0.052   <0.001∗∗∗
   TRACE (frozen)                            1.195 ± 0.026            1.014 ± 0.073          0.457 ± 0.015   <0.001∗∗∗

   RR-MoA (Top-2, frozen)                    0.680 ± 0.027            0.564 ± 0.059         0.276 ± 0.017       —

RR-MoA outperforms every baseline (p<0.001). The independent ensemble (same experts, no router)
is 36–46% worse, confirming learned routing adds value beyond diversity.
Closing the DLinear gap. DLinear (49K params, from scratch) outperforms frozen adapters in
absolute MSE (Table 4, grey rows). The gap reflects information destruction by the normalization-
encoding pipeline (k-NN and Ridge diagnostics in Appendix M), not adapter capacity. As the
diagnosis predicts, restoring raw-signal access at the expert level closes this gap: Residual-IA+
(a dual-stream architecture in which a shared NLinear raw-input branch is added to each expert’s
output; Appendix P.1, Figure P.1) matches or beats DLinear on 6/6 datasets at H=96 (up to −7.5%),
generalizing to 107/123 cells across six backbones. A self-routed variant SR-RIA+ (Appendix P.2)
achieves the same 6/6 match-or-beat with 3 outright wins; a TSFM-free Pure Raw-MLP MoE control
(Appendix P.3) localizes the TSFM’s contribution and rules out the alternative hypothesis that the
raw branch alone is doing all the work.
Rawness vs. bypass and dose-response. Replacing the router’s raw input with RevIN-normalized
input degrades MSE by 60–88% while entropy stays high (Table F.1, Appendix F), confirming
the router relies on the content of the stripped statistics, not merely the bypass path. A dose-
response interpolation xα = (1−α) xraw + α RevIN(xraw ) reveals a phase transition (Figure D.1;
150 runs): MSE degrades by +5.4% for α≤0.75 but jumps to +283% at α=1.0, while entropy
stays in [1.43, 1.57]: normalization renders routing uninformative without reducing diversity. The
phase transition threshold is quantitatively predicted at α∗ = R(D)/(R(D) + 1) ≈ 0.40 for the
median benchmark (Appendix D.1). A temporal-shuffle ablation confirms routing depends on (M, Σ)
statistics, not temporal ordering (Appendix F).
Why RevIN destroys routing: formal analysis. The experiments above converge on a single
mechanism: instance normalization strips the per-window statistics that the router uses to tell input
regimes apart. To pin this down, we ask what information the router actually has access to before and
after normalization. Each input window xi decomposes into a mean Mi = µ(xi ), a scale Σi = σ(xi ),
and a shape Si = (xi − Mi )/Σi . RevIN maps xi 7→ Si and discards (Mi , Σi ), so the question
reduces to how much routing information lives in the discarded part. The mutual-information chain
rule gives a clean answer.
Observation 1 (Routing Information Loss Under Instance Normalization). Let X be a random
input window, E ∈ {1, . . . , K} the expert assignment produced by any deterministic router, and
S = (X − M )/Σ the RevIN-normalized shape.

        (i) Exact decomposition. I(X; E) − I(S; E) = I(M, Σ; E | S) ≥ 0.
    (ii) Quantitative bound. Without any independence assumption,
                                     I(X; E) − I(S; E) ≥ I(M, Σ; E) − I(M, Σ; S),
            where ε := I(M, Σ; S) ≥ 0 captures any residual “leakage” of the stripped statistics
            through the shape. Equality holds iff (M, Σ) ⊥⊥ S | E.

Figure 5: Left: Frozen Paradox, frozen RR-MoA beats full FT by 12–79%. Right: Observation 1
validated (ρ=−0.88, p<0.002). Traffic and Solar are boundary cases.


In the full-information corner case where the router’s signal lives entirely in the stripped statistics (E
is a deterministic function of (M, Σ) and (M, Σ) ⊥⊥ S unconditionally; see Part (iii) of the proof
in Appendix D), I(X; E) − I(S; E) = H(E): RevIN destroys the entire routing distribution. We
therefore use the signal ratio R(D) = [Var(M ) + Var(Σ)]/Var(S) as a tractable monotone proxy
for the variance contribution of the stripped statistics. The bound is empirically tight: CCA gives
ε ∈ [0.09, 1.26] nats, cross-validated within ±0.26 nats by KSG [25]; a held-out classifier shows
normalization retains only 16–41% of routing entropy with bound (ii) gap averaging 0.075 nats
(Appendix L). Training α ∈ [0, 1] as a learnable parameter, gradient descent converges to α < 0.5
on all six datasets (30/30 runs; Appendix K). R(D) predicts the raw-routing benefit at Spearman
ρ=−0.88 (p<0.002, n=9; Figure 5); H(E | S) alone does not (ρ=−0.12), since routing potential
in the stripped statistics matters, not the MI loss itself. Traffic (R=0.14, ∆=+2.9%) is correctly
predicted as a boundary case; Solar (R=0.06) is the one outlier (SSR ablation +43%; Appendix F).
Full proof in Appendix D.
Cross-backbone, task and horizon generalization. RR-MoA wins all 21 MOMENT-small/large
configurations (26–79%; Table N.1), 18/18 Moirai (3–31%), and 6/6 Moirai-MoE [12] datasets
(37–90%; Table N.2): adapter-level and backbone-internal routing operate on complementary axes.
The negative controls predicted by Observation 1 (Chronos and Timer-XL [10], neither using instance
normalization) show no collapse and no RR-MoA gain (4/18 within chance; Appendix N). Top-2
sparse routing retains most of the dense gain at a fraction of the active-expert FLOPs (Appendix I);
imputation (7/8; Appendix J) and horizons H∈{96,192,336,720} (24/24; Table C.1) confirm ro-
bustness. Residual-IA+ (Appendix P.1) extends to all 6 datasets across 5 backbones and 4 horizons
(Table P.7).

5            Conclusion and Limitations
MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail for structural reasons: the normalization needed
for distribution shift also strips the per-window statistics a downstream router needs. Raw pre-
normalization routing fixes the failure and motivates Residual-IA+ ; R(D) identifies where it applies.

Implication. Routing collapse on instance-normalized backbones illustrates a broader pattern:
when a foundation model’s input pipeline destroys signal a downstream router needs, optimization-
side rescues (load balancing, z-loss, ReLU routing) cannot recover what is structurally absent: the
fix is more readily approached by restoring the missing input than by retuning the optimizer. The
same mechanism appears in a vision control (Appendix H.1); the signal-ratio predictor R(D) predicts
which datasets benefit from raw routing (Spearman ρ=−0.88, p<0.002, with a held-out boundary
case correctly flagged as low-benefit), and generalization to normalization families we did not test is
bounded by the assumptions of Observation 1.

Limitations. Three boundaries scope our claims. (i) Normalization specificity. Our diagnosis
applies to backbones with instance-level normalization (RevIN, BatchNorm, GroupNorm); backbones
without it (Chronos, Timer-XL) show no collapse and no RR-MoA gain, as Observation 1 predicts.
Extensions to attention-based or learned normalizers are not characterized. (ii) Signal-ratio boundary
cases. The signal-ratio predictor R(D) is a single monotone proxy for variance contribution; on
Solar (R=0.06) it correctly flags low benefit but is not sharp. Datasets with strong shape-statistics


                                                                        10
coupling fall outside the tight regime of the bound. (iii) Forecasting and imputation only. We
evaluate forecasting and imputation on LTSF benchmarks. Anomaly detection, classification, and
irregularly-sampled tasks are deferred to future work.

References
 [1] Ashish Vaswani, Noam Shazeer, Niki Parmar, Jakob Uszkoreit, Llion Jones, Aidan N. Gomez,
     Lukasz Kaiser, and Illia Polosukhin. Attention is all you need. In Advances in Neural Informa-

  "new_string": "MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail for structural reasons: the normalization needed for distribution shift also strips the per-window statistics a downstream router needs. Raw pre-normalization routing fixes the failure and motivates Residual-IA\\textsuperscript{+}; $R(\\mathcal{D})$ identifies where it applies. The broader pattern: when a foundation model's input pipeline destroys signal a downstream router needs, optimization-side rescues (load balancing, z-loss, ReLU routing) cannot recover what is structurally absent — the fix is restoring the missing input, not retuning the optimizer. The same mechanism appears in a vision control (Appendix~\\ref{app:vision_moe}); generalization to untested normalization families is bounded by Observation~\\ref{thm:mi_decomp}."
}
```

662	
663	\paragraph{Limitations.} Three boundaries scope our claims. \emph{(i)~Normalization specificity.} Our diagnosis applies to backbones with instance-level normalization (RevIN, BatchNorm, GroupNorm); backbones without it (Chronos, Timer-XL) show no collapse and no RR-MoA gain, as Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} predicts. Extensions to attention-based or learned normalizers are not characterized. \emph{(ii)~Signal-ratio boundary cases.} The signal-ratio predictor $R(\mathcal{D})$ is a single monotone proxy for variance contribution; on Solar ($R{=}0.06$) it correctly flags low benefit but is not sharp. Datasets with strong shape-statistics coupling fall outside the tight regime of the bound. \emph{(iii)~Forecasting and imputation only.} We evaluate forecasting and imputation on LTSF benchmarks. Anomaly detection, classification, and irregularly-sampled tasks are deferred to future work.

RR-MoA outperforms every baseline (p<0.001). The independent ensemble (same experts, no router)
is 36–46% worse, confirming learned routing adds value beyond diversity.
Closing the DLinear gap. DLinear (49K params, from scratch) outperforms frozen adapters in
absolute MSE (Table 4, grey rows). The gap reflects information destruction by the normalization-
encoding pipeline (k-NN and Ridge diagnostics in Appendix M), not adapter capacity. As the
diagnosis predicts, restoring raw-signal access at the expert level closes this gap: Residual-IA+
(a dual-stream architecture in which a shared NLinear raw-input branch is added to each expert’s
output; Appendix P.1, Figure P.1) matches or beats DLinear on 6/6 datasets at H=96 (up to −7.5%),
generalizing to 107/123 cells across six backbones. A self-routed variant SR-RIA+ (Appendix P.2)
achieves the same 6/6 match-or-beat with 3 outright wins; a TSFM-free Pure Raw-MLP MoE control
(Appendix P.3) localizes the TSFM’s contribution and rules out the alternative hypothesis that the
raw branch alone is doing all the work.
Rawness vs. bypass and dose-response. Replacing the router’s raw input with RevIN-normalized
input degrades MSE by 60–88% while entropy stays high (Table F.1, Appendix F), confirming
the router relies on the content of the stripped statistics, not merely the bypass path. A dose-
response interpolation xα = (1−α) xraw + α RevIN(xraw ) reveals a phase transition (Figure D.1;
150 runs): MSE degrades by +5.4% for α≤0.75 but jumps to +283% at α=1.0, while entropy
stays in [1.43, 1.57]: normalization renders routing uninformative without reducing diversity. The
phase transition threshold is quantitatively predicted at α∗ = R(D)/(R(D) + 1) ≈ 0.40 for the
median benchmark (Appendix D.1). A temporal-shuffle ablation confirms routing depends on (M, Σ)
statistics, not temporal ordering (Appendix F).
Why RevIN destroys routing: formal analysis. The experiments above converge on a single
mechanism: instance normalization strips the per-window statistics that the router uses to tell input
regimes apart. To pin this down, we ask what information the router actually has access to before and
after normalization. Each input window xi decomposes into a mean Mi = µ(xi ), a scale Σi = σ(xi ),
and a shape Si = (xi − Mi )/Σi . RevIN maps xi 7→ Si and discards (Mi , Σi ), so the question
reduces to how much routing information lives in the discarded part. The mutual-information chain
rule gives a clean answer.
Observation 1 (Routing Information Loss Under Instance Normalization). Let X be a random
input window, E ∈ {1, . . . , K} the expert assignment produced by any deterministic router, and
S = (X − M )/Σ the RevIN-normalized shape.

        (i) Exact decomposition. I(X; E) − I(S; E) = I(M, Σ; E | S) ≥ 0.
    (ii) Quantitative bound. Without any independence assumption,
                                     I(X; E) − I(S; E) ≥ I(M, Σ; E) − I(M, Σ; S),
            where ε := I(M, Σ; S) ≥ 0 captures any residual “leakage” of the stripped statistics
            through the shape. Equality holds iff (M, Σ) ⊥⊥ S | E.

431	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} Frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on all 6 LTSF datasets (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}). \emph{This headline gap is a two-factor contrast} (frozen+MoE vs.\ unfrozen+single-head) that decomposes into two mechanisms with very different effect sizes:
432	\emph{(M1) MoE architectural advantage (dominant, $26$--$79\%$).} The frozen five-expert mixture with learned per-sample routing provides an architectural advantage that a single unfrozen head cannot match even with full parameter access: per-sample expert selection captures input-regime structure that no single topology in our pool represents on its own (the best-fixed-adapter row of Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa} loses by $26$--$79\%$ \emph{at the same freeze level}, isolating M1 from M2).
433	\emph{(M2) Within-architecture freeze advantage (smaller, $\leq 13\%$, non-unidirectional).} Within RR-MoA itself (architecture held fixed), frozen is best on $4/6$ datasets; light unfreezing wins by up to $13\%$ on the other two. The within-architecture freeze effect is therefore real but bounded and direction-mixed; the headline $12$--$79\%$ number is dominated by M1.
434	\emph{Causal evidence for M2 specifically.} Unfreezing triggers gradient co-adaptation (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}): the dominant expert reshapes the shared backbone, collapsing routing, and freezing prevents this loop and preserves expert diversity. The Router-Detached Gradient Flow ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}) unfreezes the backbone but cuts the routing-weighted gradient feedback, degrading MSE by $+35$--$100\%$ on three datasets — a direct counterfactual on the co-adaptation term. Disabling RevIN inside MOMENT recovers full-FT performance by $54$--$61\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}), pinning the mechanism on instance normalization rather than optimization. SR-MoA (a different router architecture) independently replicates the within-architecture pattern (frozen best on $4/6$; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). Even after $90$-config hyperparameter tuning (learning rates $10^{-5}$ to $10^{-6}$, $100$ epochs, cosine schedule with $10$-epoch warmup, layerwise LR decay, weight decay sweep, gradient accumulation up to effective batch $512$), full fine-tuning still loses by $51$--$71\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}), ruling out optimization confounds.}
435	
436	\begin{proposition}[Gradient Co-Adaptation Drives Entropy Collapse]
437	\label{prop:frozen}
438	Consider a $2$-expert MoE with backbone $\mathbf{h}{=}A\mathbf{x}$, router $p{=}\sigma(\mathbf{w}^\top\mathbf{h})$, loss $\ell{=}p\,\ell_1{+}(1{-}p)\,\ell_2$. Under gradient descent on $(A,\mathbf{w})$: \textup{(i)} the dominant expert's loss decreases faster ($\dot{\ell}_k \propto {-}p_k\|\nabla_\mathbf{h}\ell_k\|^2$), and $\dot{p}$ amplifies whichever expert currently has lower loss, creating self-reinforcing collapse toward $p{\in}\{0,1\}$; \textup{(ii)} freezing $A$ removes the co-adaptation feedback through the backbone (full non-collapse additionally requires $(\ell_1{-}\ell_2)$ to flip sign across input regimes, i.e., expert specialization, which is satisfied by the topologically distinct RR-MoA pool). Proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}.

496	
497	\textbf{The principle is architecture-agnostic (SR-MoA).} To confirm the bottleneck is in the routing \emph{input} rather than the router \emph{architecture}, SR-MoA~\citep{lv2025aoe,cheng2025ermoe,liu2026routingfree} replaces the external Conv1d router with a per-expert sigmoid gate $\sigma_k(\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw})$, with no external router at all. SR-MoA \emph{outperforms} RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets (ETTh1: $0.464$; ETTm1: $0.388$; Weather: $0.222$; 252 runs total), generalizes across six backbones, and independently replicates the Frozen Paradox (frozen best on 4/6 datasets; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). That a different architecture exceeds RR-MoA while sharing only the raw-input principle confirms it is the \emph{principle}, not the specific router design, that drives the gains. The same SR-MoA architecture gating on hidden states degrades by $+87\%$ to $+768\%$ on \textbf{all 6 datasets} ($p{=}0.016$, one-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank). \textbf{Normalization-specificity:} on Timer-XL (no RevIN), the same raw-vs-hidden comparison yields only $+4$--$9\%$ ($15$--$100{\times}$ smaller than on MOMENT), confirming the degradation is normalization-induced. A third architecture-level control, \textbf{AdaMix-Raw} (Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}), replaces AdaMix's hidden-state router with a raw-input router and recovers entropy to $1.41$--$1.57$ and MSE by $41$--$88\%$ on all 12 cells. All three router instantiations (RR-MoA, SR-MoA, AdaMix-Raw) are consistent with the same diagnosis: the bottleneck is normalization, not router design. Table~\ref{tab:causal_controls} summarizes all eight controls.

494	
495	\textbf{Three architectural instantiations of one principle.} The diagnosis says: restore raw-input access wherever normalization destroyed it. The paper proposes three variants, all confirming the same diagnosis from different angles. \emph{(i)}~\textbf{RR-MoA} (\S\ref{sec:main_results}, Figure~\ref{fig:framework}, headline contribution): external Conv1d gate reads $\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw}$ and selects Top-K experts; experts read hidden states $\mathbf{H}$. \emph{(ii)}~\textbf{SR-MoA} (Self-Routed MoA, Figure~\ref{fig:srmoa_arch}, architecture-agnostic validation): no external router, each expert has its own sigmoid gate on $\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw}$. \emph{(iii)}~\textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}, Figure~\ref{fig:residual_ia_arch}, diagnosis-actionability proof): each expert combines a shared NLinear raw-input branch with a gated backbone residual, which closes the DLinear gap.
496	
497	\textbf{The principle is architecture-agnostic (SR-MoA).} To confirm the bottleneck is in the routing \emph{input} rather than the router \emph{architecture}, SR-MoA~\citep{lv2025aoe,cheng2025ermoe,liu2026routingfree} replaces the external Conv1d router with a per-expert sigmoid gate $\sigma_k(\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw})$, with no external router at all. SR-MoA \emph{outperforms} RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets (ETTh1: $0.464$; ETTm1: $0.388$; Weather: $0.222$; 252 runs total), generalizes across six backbones, and independently replicates the Frozen Paradox (frozen best on 4/6 datasets; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). That a different architecture exceeds RR-MoA while sharing only the raw-input principle confirms it is the \emph{principle}, not the specific router design, that drives the gains. A third architecture-level control, \textbf{AdaMix-Raw} (Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}), replaces AdaMix's hidden-state router with a raw-input router and recovers entropy to $1.41$--$1.57$ and MSE by $41$--$88\%$ on all 12 cells. All three router instantiations (RR-MoA, SR-MoA, AdaMix-Raw) are consistent with the same diagnosis: the bottleneck is normalization, not router design. SR-MoA \\emph{outperforms} RR-MoA by $13$--$42\\%$ across all 6 datasets (ETTh1: $0.464$; ETTm1: $0.388$; Weather: $0.222$; 252 runs total), generalizes across six backbones, and independently replicates the Frozen Paradox (frozen best on 4/6 datasets; Table~\\ref{tab:self_routed}). That a different architecture exceeds RR-MoA while sharing only the raw-input principle confirms it is the \\emph{principle}, not the specific router design, that drives the gains. The same SR-MoA architecture gating on hidden states degrades by $+87\\%$ to $+768\\%$ on \\textbf{all 6 datasets} ($p{=}0.016$, one-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank). \\textbf{Normalization-specificity:} on Timer-XL (no RevIN), the same raw-vs-hidden comparison yields only $+4$--$9\\%$ ($15$--$100{\\times}$ smaller than on MOMENT), confirming the degradation is normalization-induced. A third architecture-level control, \\textbf{AdaMix-Raw} (Appendix~\\ref{app:adamix_details}), replaces AdaMix's hidden-state router with a raw-input router and recovers entropy to $1.41$--$1.57$ and MSE by $41$--$88\\%$ on all 12 cells. All three router instantiations (RR-MoA, SR-MoA, AdaMix-Raw) are consistent with the same diagnosis: the bottleneck is normalization, not router design. Table~\\ref{tab:causal_controls} summarizes all eight controls.",
  "new_string": "\\textbf{The principle is architecture-agnostic.} The diagnosis predicts the same fix should work for any router that reads raw input. We validate this with three router-level controls. \\textbf{SR-MoA}~\\citep{lv2025aoe,cheng2025ermoe,liu2026routingfree} (Self-Routed MoA, Figure~\\ref{fig:srmoa_arch}) drops the external router entirely: each expert has its own sigmoid gate $\\sigma_k(\\mathbf{X}_\\mathrm{raw})$. SR-MoA \\emph{outperforms} RR-MoA by $13$--$42\\%$ across all 6 datasets ($252$ runs, six backbones) and replicates the Frozen Paradox (frozen best on 4/6; Table~\\ref{tab:self_routed}). Gating the same SR-MoA architecture on hidden states degrades by $+87$--$768\\%$ on all 6 datasets ($p{=}0.016$); on Timer-XL (no RevIN) the same raw-vs-hidden comparison yields only $+4$--$9\\%$ ($15$--$100{\\times}$ smaller), confirming the effect is normalization-induced. \\textbf{AdaMix-Raw} (Appendix~\\ref{app:adamix_details}) swaps AdaMix's hidden-state router for a raw-input router and recovers entropy to $1.41$--$1.57$ and MSE by $41$--$88\\%$ on all 12 cells. \\textbf{Residual-IA\\textsuperscript{+}} (Appendix~\\ref{app:gate_pathology}, Figure~\\ref{fig:residual_ia_arch}) extends the principle to the expert level via a shared NLinear raw branch + gated backbone residual, closing the DLinear gap. Table~\\ref{tab:causal_controls} summarizes all eight controls."
}
